---------- turn 12 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF23, F20.9, F25.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess the main thing is my talking, it gets all mixed up, and people keep telling me they can’t follow me. Like I’ll be trying to explain something simple, you know, about the keys, because the keys matter, and then somehow I’m talking about jail, and then the prayer rug comes into it, and then chess pieces, and in my head it feels connected but when it comes out people look at me like I’m not making sense.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I try to slow down and say it right, but then I lose the start of what I was saying, or I jump to another part because that part seems important too. So I came in because it’s been bad enough that I can tell other people are getting confused by me, and I don’t know how to straighten it out when I’m speaking.\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any substances, like alcohol or drugs, around the times when your talking gets mixed up?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track like that, like writing down what was happening before my talking got mixed up or anything. When people tell me I’m not making sense, I’m usually already in the middle of trying to explain something, so I’m not thinking, “What did I have earlier?” or connecting it to that.\n\nWhen you say substances, do you mean anything at all, like even small stuff, or do you mean more like being high or drunk? Because I don’t want to say the wrong thing. I just haven’t noticed a clear pattern where it’s like, “Oh, every time this happens, it’s because of that.” It feels more like the talking just starts going sideways, and then everybody’s reacting to me, and I’m trying to fix the sentence while it’s already coming out wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you used any alcohol or drugs in the past month?\n\nPatient: In the past month… I’m not sure how to give you a solid answer. I haven’t been keeping a list or anything, and my days sort of run together sometimes. If you mean like regular drinking or drugs where I know, “Yes, I did that,” I don’t have a clear thing to point to.\n\nI don’t want to say no like I’m making a sworn statement and then remember something small later, but I also don’t have something specific to tell you, like a time or amount. I just haven’t been tracking it that way, and I haven’t noticed some obvious pattern where alcohol or drugs are the reason things get mixed up.\n\nDoctor: Has your talking always been like this, or is this something that has started happening more recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was always this bad, no. I mean, I’ve always maybe talked a lot, maybe gone around the block to get to the point, people have told me that before, but this thing where I start on one thing and then it turns into another and another… that feels more like what’s been bringing me here.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to say something plain, just about the keys, and why the keys were important, and then while I’m saying it there’s this other connection in my head, like jail, because keys and locks and being stuck, and then somehow I’m talking about a prayer rug, and then chess pieces, and I can see little pieces of why they belong together, but when I look at the other person’s face I can tell they’re lost. They’ll say, “What are you talking about?” or “Slow down,” and then I get embarrassed and try to back up, but backing up makes it worse because now I’ve got too many beginnings.\n\nSo… more recently it’s been noticeable, I guess. Noticeable enough that other people cannot follow me, and I can tell I’m not getting it out right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or panicked when you realize people are having trouble following what you're saying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m not sure if I’d call it panic exactly. I mean, I do get… something, when I see that look on someone’s face, like they’re waiting for me to land the plane and I’m still circling around. It makes me more aware of myself, and then I start trying to fix it while I’m still talking, which doesn’t always help.\n\nAnxious, maybe, yes, in the sense that I feel pressure. Like, “Okay, say it right, say it normal, don’t make them think you’re just rambling.” But I don’t know if it’s panic like a full-on thing. It’s more like embarrassment and frustration mixed together. I can tell they’re not following me, and then I get stuck trying to explain the explanation, and that makes me sound even more tangled.\n\nSometimes I want to slow down, but then if I slow down too much I lose the piece I was trying to say. So I’m kind of choosing between losing the thought or saying it wrong. That’s the part that gets me worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices or sounds that other people can't hear when you're having trouble getting your thoughts out?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, sometimes. I don’t always know how to say that part without sounding like I’m making it bigger than it is, but sometimes when I’m already trying to get my words straight, I’ll hear a man’s voice.\n\nIt’s not like a thought exactly. That’s the thing. It doesn’t feel like me thinking it. It sounds like it’s coming from somewhere in the building, like the vents, or once I thought it was from the mop closet, like somebody was in there but when I checked there wasn’t anybody. And it’ll say my name, or it’ll say something like I ruined everything.\n\nAnd when it happens, it sounds real. I mean real like if you were standing right beside me and said something. So then I get distracted because I’m trying to listen and also trying not to look like I’m listening to something nobody else hears, and then my talking gets worse because now I’m answering you, but part of me is checking the room, checking the vent, wondering if somebody’s messing with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to organize your thoughts even when you're not trying to explain something to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, it isn’t only when I’m talking to somebody. That’s the part that scares me a little, because even when I’m just by myself, like doing something simple, my mind starts lining things up in a way that feels connected to me, but then if I try to hold onto it, it gets all mixed up.\n\nLike the keys thing I mentioned — I’ll be thinking about keys because I need the keys for work, and then it’s locks, and then jail, because locks mean jail, and then somehow I’m thinking about a prayer rug, and then chess pieces, and I can kind of see the path between them in my head. It’s not just random to me while it’s happening. But if I had to say it out loud, people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. And sometimes even inside my own head, I lose which part was the main point.\n\nIt’s like I have too many beginnings and no straight hallway to walk down. I’m trying to organize it, but it keeps branching off. So yes, even before I open my mouth, it can already be tangled.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your daily activities or work performance over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, not in a small way, either. It’s been getting harder to keep up with normal things, like work and going places I used to go without thinking so much about it.\n\nWith the cleaning jobs, I’ve missed shifts. Sometimes I don’t get there, or I get there and I’m not doing things the way they want, or people say they can’t count on me the same way. And with cleaning, you know, people notice if something’s not done right. It’s not like you can hide it. Floors aren’t finished, trash isn’t handled, bathrooms aren’t stocked right, and then somebody has to come behind you. I’ve lost some of that work because of it. People stop calling, or they say it’s better if they use somebody else because they need someone reliable.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I stopped going to the mosque like I used to. I used to be more regular, and I’d see people, talk a little, be part of things. Now I stay away more. Same with family gatherings. I don’t go much, or I leave early, because it feels like people can’t follow me or they get uncomfortable, and then I feel like I’m making everybody tired. Some of them have said things like they don’t understand what I’m trying to say, or they don’t know if they can rely on me to show up or help with something.\n\nAnd… there have been times where it got bad enough that other people had to call for emergency help. I don’t like saying that. It makes me feel ashamed, like I’m some kind of problem people have to manage. But yes, over the past few months, my daily life and work have definitely changed. I’m not functioning the way I was.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe things that, in hindsight, might not be true, like thinking someone is out to get you or that you have special powers?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’ve had that. I don’t like calling it “special powers” or anything like that, because that sounds like I’m trying to make myself important. It’s more like I get sure people are signaling about me.\n\nThere was this thing at the building where I work, or worked some of the time, where I thought the building people had marked me somehow. Like they knew something about me and were passing it around without saying it straight. The hallway lights felt like part of it — like if one light flickered or stayed on a certain way, that meant something, like a warning. And then the little looks people gave each other, or somebody stopping talking when I came near… it felt like they were warning each other about me.\n\nMy supervisor even showed me the camera, like, “Look, nobody’s doing anything, nobody’s following you, nothing’s happening there.” And I could see what he was trying to show me. I could see the screen. But inside I still felt sure. That’s the part that embarrasses me, because even with him showing me nothing was on the camera, I didn’t believe it the way I should have. I kept thinking maybe the camera didn’t catch the right part, or they knew where the camera was, or it was happening in the lights and the way people looked, not something obvious.\n\nLater, when I’m calmer, I can say maybe that wasn’t true. But at the time it felt completely real, like I was the only one noticing what was actually going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are often disjointed and difficult to put into words, even when you're not trying to explain something to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly what I mean. It’s not only when I’m trying to explain it to somebody else — it starts before that. The words and thoughts kind of get tangled up inside, and then when I try to speak, it comes out all sideways.\n\nLike I’ll start with something simple, like the keys, because I need my keys or I’m thinking about getting into work, and then my mind goes from keys to locks, and locks to jail, and then I’m thinking about a prayer rug, and then somehow chess pieces come into it. And in my head, while it’s happening, it feels like there is a connection. I can feel the connection, almost like I’m following a thread. But when I say it out loud, people look at me like I skipped half the steps, or they tell me, “I can’t follow what you’re saying.”\n\nThat’s what brings me here, really. My talking gets all mixed up. I try to explain one thing, and by the time it comes out, it’s turned into three or four other things, and even I sometimes can’t put it back in the right order. It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to confuse people. I’m trying to say it right. It just doesn’t stay straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your thoughts are as disjointed and hard to follow when you're alone and not trying to talk to anyone?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, it is like that even when I’m by myself. That’s the part that makes it hard to explain, because it’s not only me failing to talk right in front of people. It starts in my head first.\n\nI’ll be sitting there alone, not even saying anything, and I’m trying to think about one thing — like, okay, where are my keys, or I have to get ready — and then it slides. Keys make me think of locks, and then locks make me think of jail, and then somehow I’m thinking about a prayer rug, and then chess pieces. And while it’s happening, it feels like there’s a reason those things are connected. It doesn’t feel random right away. It feels like I’m following something.\n\nBut then if I try to say it out loud later, it comes out all mixed up. That’s what brings me here, really — my talking gets tangled. I try to explain about the keys, then the jail, then the prayer rug, then chess pieces, and people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. And honestly, sometimes when I’m alone, even I lose the thread and can’t get it back straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble sticking to one topic when you're thinking to yourself, without trying to explain it to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when I’m not talking to anybody, it doesn’t stay on one thing. I’ll be trying to think about something ordinary, like where my keys are, and then it’s like my mind jumps tracks. Keys turns into locks, then I’m thinking about jail, then somehow a prayer rug comes in, then chess pieces, and I’m sitting there like… wait, how did I get over here?\n\nAt the time it feels connected, like there’s a path I’m following, but I can’t always explain the path after. And then when I do try to tell somebody, that’s when it really shows. What brings me here is my talking gets all mixed up. I try to explain about the keys, then the jail, then the prayer rug, then chess pieces, and people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. I’m not trying to wander off the subject, it just won’t stay in one place.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It just doesn’t stay straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your thoughts are as disjointed and hard to follow when you're alone and not trying to talk to anyone?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, it is like that even when I’m by myself. It starts in my head first.\n\nI’ll be sitting there alone, not even saying anything, and I’m trying to think about one thing — like, okay, where are my keys, or I have to get ready — and then it slides. Keys make me think of locks, and then locks make me think of jail, and then somehow I’m thinking about a prayer rug, and then chess pieces. And while it’s happening, it feels like there’s a reason those things are connected. It doesn’t feel random right away. It feels like I’m following something.\n\nBut then if I try to say it out loud later, it comes out all mixed up. That’s what brings me here, really — my talking gets tangled. Yes… yes, even when nobody’s asking me anything, it can still be like that. It’s not only because I’m nervous or because someone is standing there waiting for me to explain it. It’s already mixed up before I open my mouth.

Like I’ll be thinking about something simple, just one thing, like the keys, and then it doesn’t stay there. It goes from keys to jail, or locks and jail, and then somehow I’m thinking about a prayer rug, and then chess pieces, and while it’s happening I feel like there’s a connection. But then if I try to put it into words, it comes out wrong, like I’m jumping all over.

That’s really why I came in. My talking gets all mixed up. I try to explain about the keys, then the jail, then the prayer rug, then the chess pieces, and people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. And I can see it on their faces sometimes, like I lost them, but I don’t know exactly where I lost them because in my head it felt like it made sense a second before.

  {
    "role": "system",

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF23, F20.9, F25.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Like I’ll be trying to explain something simple, you know, about the keys, because the keys matter, and then somehow I’m talking about jail, and then the prayer rug comes into it, and then chess pieces, and in my head it feels connected but when it comes out people look at me like I’m not making sense.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I try to slow down and say it right, but then I lose the start of what I was saying, or I jump to another part because that part seems important too. So I came in because it’s been bad enough that I can tell other people are getting confused by me, and I don’t know how to straighten it out when I’m speaking.\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any substances, like alcohol or drugs, around the times when your talking gets mixed up?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track like that, like writing down what was happening before my talking got mixed up or anything. When people tell me I’m not making sense, I’m usually already in the middle of trying to explain something, so I’m not thinking, “What did I have earlier?” or connecting it to that.\n\nWhen you say substances, do you mean anything at all, like even small stuff, or do you mean more like being high or drunk? Because I don’t want to say the wrong thing. I just haven’t noticed a clear pattern where it’s like, “Oh, every time this happens, it’s because of that.” It feels more like the talking just starts going sideways, and then everybody’s reacting to me, and I’m trying to fix the sentence while it’s already coming out wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you used any alcohol or drugs in the past month?\n\nPatient: In the past month… I’m not sure how to give you a solid answer. I haven’t been keeping a list or anything, and my days sort of run together sometimes. I just haven’t been tracking it that way, and I haven’t noticed some obvious pattern where alcohol or drugs are the reason things get mixed up.\n\nDoctor: Has your talking always been like this, or is this something that has started happening more recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was always this bad, no. I mean, I’ve always maybe talked a lot, maybe gone around the block to get to the point, people have told me that before, but this thing where I start on one thing and then it turns into another and another… that feels more like what’s been bringing me here.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to say something plain, just about the keys, and why the keys were important, and then while I’m saying it there’s this other connection in my head, like jail, because keys and locks and being stuck, and then somehow I’m talking about a prayer rug, and then chess pieces, and I can see little pieces of why they belong together, but when I look at the other person’s face I can tell they’re lost. They’ll say, “What are you talking about?” or “Slow down,” and then I get embarrassed and try to back up, but backing up makes it worse because now I’ve got too many beginnings.\n\nSo… more recently it’s been noticeable, I guess. Noticeable enough that other people cannot follow me, and I can tell I’m not getting it out right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or panicked when you realize people are having trouble following what you're saying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m not sure if I’d call it panic exactly. I mean, I do get… something, when I see that look on someone’s face, like they’re waiting for me to land the plane and I’m still circling around. It makes me more aware of myself, and then I start trying to fix it while I’m still talking, which doesn’t always help.\n\nAnxious, maybe, yes, in the sense that I feel pressure. Like, “Okay, say it right, say it normal, don’t make them think you’re just rambling.” But I don’t know if it’s panic like a full-on thing. It’s more like embarrassment and frustration mixed together. I can tell they’re not following me, and then I get stuck trying to explain the explanation, and that makes me sound even more tangled.\n\nSometimes I want to slow down, but then if I slow down too much I lose the piece I was trying to say. So I’m kind of choosing between losing the thought or saying it wrong. That’s the part that gets me worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices or sounds that other people can't hear when you're having trouble getting your thoughts out?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, sometimes. I don’t always know how to say that part without sounding like I’m making it bigger than it is, but sometimes when I’m already trying to get my words straight, I’ll hear a man’s voice.\n\nIt’s not like a thought exactly. That’s the thing. It doesn’t feel like me thinking it. It sounds like it’s coming from somewhere in the building, like the vents, or once I thought it was from the mop closet, like somebody was in there but when I checked there wasn’t anybody. And it’ll say my name, or it’ll say something like I ruined everything.\n\nAnd when it happens, it sounds real. I mean real like if you were standing right beside me and said something. So then I get distracted because I’m trying to listen and also trying not to look like I’m listening to something nobody else hears, and then my talking gets worse because now I’m answering you, but part of me is checking the room, checking the vent, wondering if somebody’s messing with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to organize your thoughts even when you're not trying to explain something to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, it isn’t only when I’m talking to somebody. That’s the part that scares me a little, because even when I’m just by myself, like doing something simple, my mind starts lining things up in a way that feels connected to me, but then if I try to hold onto it, it gets all mixed up.\n\nLike the keys thing I mentioned — I’ll be thinking about keys because I need the keys for work, and then it’s locks, and then jail, because locks mean jail, and then somehow I’m thinking about a prayer rug, and then chess pieces, and I can kind of see the path between them in my head. It’s not just random to me while it’s happening. But if I had to say it out loud, people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. And sometimes even inside my own head, I lose which part was the main point.\n\nIt’s like I have too many beginnings and no straight hallway to walk down. I’m trying to organize it, but it keeps branching off. So yes, even before I open my mouth, it can already be tangled.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your daily activities or work performance over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, not in a small way, either. It’s been getting harder to keep up with normal things, like work and going places I used to go without thinking so much about it.\n\nWith the cleaning jobs, I’ve missed shifts. Sometimes I don’t get there, or I get there and I’m not doing things the way they want, or people say they can’t count on me the same way. And with cleaning, you know, people notice if something’s not done right. It’s not like you can hide it. Floors aren’t finished, trash isn’t handled, bathrooms aren’t stocked right, and then somebody has to come behind you. I’ve lost some of that work because of it. People stop calling, or they say it’s better if they use somebody else because they need someone reliable.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I stopped going to the mosque like I used to. I used to be more regular, and I’d see people, talk a little, be part of things. Now I stay away more. Same with family gatherings. I don’t go much, or I leave early, because it feels like people can’t follow me or they get uncomfortable, and then I feel like I’m making everybody tired. Some of them have said things like they don’t understand what I’m trying to say, or they don’t know if they can rely on me to show up or help with something.\n\nAnd… there have been times where it got bad enough that other people had to call for emergency help. I don’t like saying that. It makes me feel ashamed, like I’m some kind of problem people have to manage. But yes, over the past few months, my daily life and work have definitely changed. I’m not functioning the way I was.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe things that, in hindsight, might not be true, like thinking someone is out to get you or that you have special powers?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’ve had that. I don’t like calling it “special powers” or anything like that, because that sounds like I’m trying to make myself important. It’s more like I get sure people are signaling about me.\n\nThere was this thing at the building where I work, or worked some of the time, where I thought the building people had marked me somehow. Like they knew something about me and were passing it around without saying it straight. The hallway lights felt like part of it — like if one light flickered or stayed on a certain way, that meant something, like a warning. And then the little looks people gave each other, or somebody stopping talking when I came near… it felt like they were warning each other about me.\n\nMy supervisor even showed me the camera, like, “Look, nobody’s doing anything, nobody’s following you, nothing’s happening there.” And I could see what he was trying to show me. I could see the screen. But inside I still felt sure. That’s the part that embarrasses me, because even with him showing me nothing was on the camera, I didn’t believe it the way I should have. I kept thinking maybe the camera didn’t catch the right part, or they knew where the camera was, or it was happening in the lights and the way people looked, not something obvious.\n\nLater, when I’m calmer, I can say maybe that wasn’t true. But at the time it felt completely real, like I was the only one noticing what was actually going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are often disjointed and difficult to put into words, even when you're not trying to explain something to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly what I mean. It’s not only when I’m trying to explain it to somebody else — it starts before that. The words and thoughts kind of get tangled up inside, and then when I try to speak, it comes out all sideways.\n\nLike I’ll start with something simple, like the keys, because I need my keys or I’m thinking about getting into work, and then my mind goes from keys to locks, and locks to jail, and then I’m thinking about a prayer rug, and then somehow chess pieces come into it. And in my head, while it’s happening, it feels like there is a connection. I can feel the connection, almost like I’m following a thread. But when I say it out loud, people look at me like I skipped half the steps, or they tell me, “I can’t follow what you’re saying.”\n\nThat’s what brings me here, really. My talking gets all mixed up. I try to explain one thing, and by the time it comes out, it’s turned into three or four other things, and even I sometimes can’t put it back in the right order. It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to confuse people. I’m trying to say it right. It just doesn’t stay straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your thoughts are as disjointed and hard to follow when you're alone and not trying to talk to anyone?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, it is like that even when I’m by myself. That’s the part that makes it hard to explain, because it’s not only me failing to talk right in front of people. It starts in my head first.\n\nI’ll be sitting there alone, not even saying anything, and I’m trying to think about one thing — like, okay, where are my keys, or I have to get ready — and then it slides. Keys make me think of locks, and then locks make me think of jail, and then somehow I’m thinking about a prayer rug, and then chess pieces. And while it’s happening, it feels like there’s a reason those things are connected. It doesn’t feel random right away. It feels like I’m following something.\n\nBut then if I try to say it out loud later, it comes out all mixed up. That’s what brings me here, really — my talking gets tangled. I try to explain about the keys, then the jail, then the prayer rug, then chess pieces, and people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. And honestly, sometimes when I’m alone, even I lose the thread and can’t get it back straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble sticking to one topic when you're thinking to yourself, without trying to explain it to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when I’m not talking to anybody, it doesn’t stay on one thing. I’ll be trying to think about something ordinary, like where my keys are, and then it’s like my mind jumps tracks. Keys turns into locks, then I’m thinking about jail, then somehow a prayer rug comes in, then chess pieces, and I’m sitting there like… wait, how did I get over here?\n\nAt the time it feels connected, like there’s a path I’m following, but I can’t always explain the path after. And then when I do try to tell somebody, that’s when it really shows. What brings me here is my talking gets all mixed up. I try to explain about the keys, then the jail, then the prayer rug, then chess pieces, and people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. I’m not trying to wander off the subject, it just won’t stay in one place.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are as disjointed when you're not feeling pressured to explain them to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, even when nobody’s asking me anything, it can still be like that. It’s not only because I’m nervous or because someone is standing there waiting for me to explain it. It’s already mixed up before I open my mouth.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about something simple, just one thing, like the keys, and then it doesn’t stay there. It goes from keys to jail, or locks and jail, and then somehow I’m thinking about a prayer rug, and then chess pieces, and while it’s happening I feel like there’s a connection. But then if I try to put it into words, it comes out wrong, like I’m jumping all over.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. My talking gets all mixed up. I try to explain about the keys, then the jail, then the prayer rug, then the chess pieces, and people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. And I can see it on their faces sometimes, like I lost them, but I don’t know exactly where I lost them because in my head it felt like it made sense a second before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Sometimes I guess I can sound normal for a little bit, especially if it’s something simple, like yes or no, or if I’m just saying I cleaned this hallway or I need to go get something. But it doesn’t feel dependable. Like I can start out organized, and then if I have to explain more than one piece of it, it starts getting tangled.

That’s really what brings me here. My talking gets all mixed up. I try to explain something, and it starts with the keys, then I’m talking about the jail, then the prayer rug, then chess pieces, and people are looking at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying. So yes, maybe there are little stretches where I seem okay, but then when I have to say what I mean, it can fall apart pretty fast.

That’s the thing. It doesn’t feel like me thinking it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. I mean, I know how it sounds when I say it, because other people don’t see it the same way, but it still feels true to me.

At work, in the building, I felt like the building people had marked me somehow. Not marked like with ink, I don’t mean that exactly, but like… I was singled out. Like they knew something about me or had decided something about me, and then the hallway lights and the way people looked at me were being used like signals. Little warnings. Like one light would flicker, or come on at a certain time, and then somebody would give me this quick look, not a normal look, more like they were passing it along.

My supervisor tried to show me the camera, like, “Look, nothing is happening, nobody is doing anything.” And I looked at it, I did. I stood there and watched what he was showing me. But even with that, I still felt sure there was something going on that just wasn’t showing up on the camera. Like the camera doesn’t catch what people mean by a look, or how the lights are timed, or the little things people do when they’re warning each other. So other people might say, “No, that’s not real,” but inside me it still felt real. It still does sometimes when I think about it.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess the main thing is my talking, it gets all mixed up, and people keep telling me they can’t follow me. Noticeable enough that other people cannot follow me, and I can tell I’m not getting it out right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or panicked when you realize people are having trouble following what you're saying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m not sure if I’d call it panic exactly. I mean, I do get… something, when I see that look on someone’s face, like they’re waiting for me to land the plane and I’m still circling around. It’s more like embarrassment and frustration mixed together. That’s the part that gets me worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices or sounds that other people can't hear when you're having trouble getting your thoughts out?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, sometimes. I don’t always know how to say that part without sounding like I’m making it bigger than it is, but sometimes when I’m already trying to get my words straight, I’ll hear a man’s voice.\n\nIt’s not like a thought exactly. That’s the thing. It doesn’t feel like me thinking it. It sounds like it’s coming from somewhere in the building, like the vents, or once I thought it was from the mop closet, like somebody was in there but when I checked there wasn’t anybody. And it’ll say my name, or it’ll say something like I ruined everything.\n\nAnd when it happens, it sounds real. I mean real like if you were standing right beside me and said something. So then I get distracted because I’m trying to listen and also trying not to look like I’m listening to something nobody else hears, and then my talking gets worse because now I’m answering you, but part of me is checking the room, checking the vent, wondering if somebody’s messing with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to organize your thoughts even when you're not trying to explain something to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, it isn’t only when I’m talking to somebody. That’s the part that scares me a little, because even when I’m just by myself, like doing something simple, my mind starts lining things up in a way that feels connected to me, but then if I try to hold onto it, it gets all mixed up.\n\nLike the keys thing I mentioned — I’ll be thinking about keys because I need the keys for work, and then it’s locks, and then jail, because locks mean jail, and then somehow I’m thinking about a prayer rug, and then chess pieces, and I can kind of see the path between them in my head. It’s not just random to me while it’s happening. But if I had to say it out loud, people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. And sometimes even inside my own head, I lose which part was the main point.\n\nIt’s like I have too many beginnings and no straight hallway to walk down. I’m trying to organize it, but it keeps branching off. So yes, even before I open my mouth, it can already be tangled.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your daily activities or work performance over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, not in a small way, either. It’s been getting harder to keep up with normal things, like work and going places I used to go without thinking so much about it.\n\nWith the cleaning jobs, I’ve missed shifts. Sometimes I don’t get there, or I get there and I’m not doing things the way they want, or people say they can’t count on me the same way. And with cleaning, you know, people notice if something’s not done right. It’s not like you can hide it. Floors aren’t finished, trash isn’t handled, bathrooms aren’t stocked right, and then somebody has to come behind you. I’ve lost some of that work because of it. People stop calling, or they say it’s better if they use somebody else because they need someone reliable.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I stopped going to the mosque like I used to. I used to be more regular, and I’d see people, talk a little, be part of things. Now I stay away more. Same with family gatherings. I don’t go much, or I leave early, because it feels like people can’t follow me or they get uncomfortable, and then I feel like I’m making everybody tired. Some of them have said things like they don’t understand what I’m trying to say, or they don’t know if they can rely on me to show up or help with something.\n\nAnd… there have been times where it got bad enough that other people had to call for emergency help. I don’t like saying that. It makes me feel ashamed, like I’m some kind of problem people have to manage. But yes, over the past few months, my daily life and work have definitely changed. I’m not functioning the way I was.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe things that, in hindsight, might not be true, like thinking someone is out to get you or that you have special powers?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’ve had that. I don’t like calling it “special powers” or anything like that, because that sounds like I’m trying to make myself important. It’s more like I get sure people are signaling about me.\n\nThere was this thing at the building where I work, or worked some of the time, where I thought the building people had marked me somehow. Like they knew something about me and were passing it around without saying it straight. The hallway lights felt like part of it — like if one light flickered or stayed on a certain way, that meant something, like a warning. And then the little looks people gave each other, or somebody stopping talking when I came near… it felt like they were warning each other about me.\n\nMy supervisor even showed me the camera, like, “Look, nobody’s doing anything, nobody’s following you, nothing’s happening there.” And I could see what he was trying to show me. I could see the screen. But inside I still felt sure. That’s the part that embarrasses me, because even with him showing me nothing was on the camera, I didn’t believe it the way I should have. I kept thinking maybe the camera didn’t catch the right part, or they knew where the camera was, or it was happening in the lights and the way people looked, not something obvious.\n\nLater, when I’m calmer, I can say maybe that wasn’t true. But at the time it felt completely real, like I was the only one noticing what was actually going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are often disjointed and difficult to put into words, even when you're not trying to explain something to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly what I mean. It’s not only when I’m trying to explain it to somebody else — it starts before that. And in my head, while it’s happening, it feels like there is a connection. I can feel the connection, almost like I’m following a thread. But when I say it out loud, people look at me like I skipped half the steps, or they tell me, “I can’t follow what you’re saying.”\n\nThat’s what brings me here, really. My talking gets all mixed up. I try to explain one thing, and by the time it comes out, it’s turned into three or four other things, and even I sometimes can’t put it back in the right order. It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to confuse people. I’m trying to say it right. It just doesn’t stay straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your thoughts are as disjointed and hard to follow when you're alone and not trying to talk to anyone?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, it is like that even when I’m by myself. And while it’s happening, it feels like there’s a reason those things are connected. It doesn’t feel random right away. It feels like I’m following something.\n\nBut then if I try to say it out loud later, it comes out all mixed up. That’s what brings me here, really — my talking gets tangled. I try to explain about the keys, then the jail, then the prayer rug, then chess pieces, and people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. And honestly, sometimes when I’m alone, even I lose the thread and can’t get it back straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble sticking to one topic when you're thinking to yourself, without trying to explain it to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I’ve always maybe talked a lot, maybe gone around the block to get to the point, people have told me that before, but this thing where I start on one thing and then it turns into another and another… that feels more like what’s been bringing me here.\n\nLike I’ll be trying to say something plain, just about the keys, and why the keys were important, and then while I’m saying it there’s this other connection in my head, like jail, because keys and locks and being stuck, and then somehow I’m talking about a prayer rug, and then chess pieces, and I can see little pieces of why they belong together, but when I look at the other person’s face I can tell they’re lost. They’ll say, “What are you talking about?” or “Slow down,” and then I get embarrassed and try to back up, but backing up makes it worse because now I’ve got too many beginnings.\n\nSo… more recently it’s been noticeable, I guess. Noticeable enough that other people cannot follow me, and I can tell I’m not getting it out right.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or panicked when you realize people are having trouble following what you're saying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m not sure if I’d call it panic exactly. I mean, I do get… something, when I see that look on someone’s face, like they’re waiting for me to land the plane and I’m still circling around. It makes me more aware of myself, and then I start trying to fix it while I’m still talking, which doesn’t always help.\n\nAnxious, maybe, yes, in the sense that I feel pressure. Like, “Okay, say it right, say it normal, don’t make them think you’re just rambling.” But I don’t know if it’s panic like a full-on thing. It’s more like embarrassment and frustration mixed together. I can tell they’re not following me, and then I get stuck trying to explain the explanation, and that makes me sound even more tangled.\n\nSometimes I want to slow down, but then if I slow down too much I lose the piece I was trying to say. So I’m kind of choosing between losing the thought or saying it wrong. That’s the part that gets me worked up.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever hear voices or sounds that other people can't hear when you're having trouble getting your thoughts out?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, sometimes. I don’t always know how to say that part without sounding like I’m making it bigger than it is, but sometimes when I’m already trying to get my words straight, I’ll hear a man’s voice.\n\nIt’s not like a thought exactly. That’s the thing. It doesn’t feel like me thinking it. It sounds like it’s coming from somewhere in the building, like the vents, or once I thought it was from the mop closet, like somebody was in there but when I checked there wasn’t anybody. And it’ll say my name, or it’ll say something like I ruined everything.\n\nAnd when it happens, it sounds real. I mean real like if you were standing right beside me and said something. So then I get distracted because I’m trying to listen and also trying not to look like I’m listening to something nobody else hears, and then my talking gets worse because now I’m answering you, but part of me is checking the room, checking the vent, wondering if somebody’s messing with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to organize your thoughts even when you're not trying to explain something to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, it isn’t only when I’m talking to somebody. That’s the part that scares me a little, because even when I’m just by myself, like doing something simple, my mind starts lining things up in a way that feels connected to me, but then if I try to hold onto it, it gets all mixed up.\n\nLike the keys thing I mentioned — I’ll be thinking about keys because I need the keys for work, and then it’s locks, and then jail, because locks mean jail, and then somehow I’m thinking about a prayer rug, and then chess pieces, and I can kind of see the path between them in my head. It’s not just random to me while it’s happening. But if I had to say it out loud, people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. And sometimes even inside my own head, I lose which part was the main point.\n\nIt’s like I have too many beginnings and no straight hallway to walk down. I’m trying to organize it, but it keeps branching off. So yes, even before I open my mouth, it can already be tangled.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your daily activities or work performance over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. I mean, not in a small way, either. It’s been getting harder to keep up with normal things, like work and going places I used to go without thinking so much about it.\n\nWith the cleaning jobs, I’ve missed shifts. Sometimes I don’t get there, or I get there and I’m not doing things the way they want, or people say they can’t count on me the same way. And with cleaning, you know, people notice if something’s not done right. It’s not like you can hide it. Floors aren’t finished, trash isn’t handled, bathrooms aren’t stocked right, and then somebody has to come behind you. I’ve lost some of that work because of it. People stop calling, or they say it’s better if they use somebody else because they need someone reliable.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I stopped going to the mosque like I used to. I used to be more regular, and I’d see people, talk a little, be part of things. Now I stay away more. Same with family gatherings. I don’t go much, or I leave early, because it feels like people can’t follow me or they get uncomfortable, and then I feel like I’m making everybody tired. Some of them have said things like they don’t understand what I’m trying to say, or they don’t know if they can rely on me to show up or help with something.\n\nAnd… there have been times where it got bad enough that other people had to call for emergency help. I don’t like saying that. It makes me feel ashamed, like I’m some kind of problem people have to manage. But yes, over the past few months, my daily life and work have definitely changed. I’m not functioning the way I was.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe things that, in hindsight, might not be true, like thinking someone is out to get you or that you have special powers?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’ve had that. I don’t like calling it “special powers” or anything like that, because that sounds like I’m trying to make myself important. It’s more like I get sure people are signaling about me.\n\nThere was this thing at the building where I work, or worked some of the time, where I thought the building people had marked me somehow. Like they knew something about me and were passing it around without saying it straight. The hallway lights felt like part of it — like if one light flickered or stayed on a certain way, that meant something, like a warning. And then the little looks people gave each other, or somebody stopping talking when I came near… it felt like they were warning each other about me.\n\nMy supervisor even showed me the camera, like, “Look, nobody’s doing anything, nobody’s following you, nothing’s happening there.” And I could see what he was trying to show me. I could see the screen. But inside I still felt sure. That’s the part that embarrasses me, because even with him showing me nothing was on the camera, I didn’t believe it the way I should have. I kept thinking maybe the camera didn’t catch the right part, or they knew where the camera was, or it was happening in the lights and the way people looked, not something obvious.\n\nLater, when I’m calmer, I can say maybe that wasn’t true. But at the time it felt completely real, like I was the only one noticing what was actually going on.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are often disjointed and difficult to put into words, even when you're not trying to explain something to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly what I mean. It’s not only when I’m trying to explain it to somebody else — it starts before that. The words and thoughts kind of get tangled up inside, and then when I try to speak, it comes out all sideways.\n\nLike I’ll start with something simple, like the keys, because I need my keys or I’m thinking about getting into work, and then my mind goes from keys to locks, and locks to jail, and then I’m thinking about a prayer rug, and then somehow chess pieces come into it. And in my head, while it’s happening, it feels like there is a connection. I can feel the connection, almost like I’m following a thread. But when I say it out loud, people look at me like I skipped half the steps, or they tell me, “I can’t follow what you’re saying.”\n\nThat’s what brings me here, really. My talking gets all mixed up. I try to explain one thing, and by the time it comes out, it’s turned into three or four other things, and even I sometimes can’t put it back in the right order. It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to confuse people. I’m trying to say it right. It just doesn’t stay straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your thoughts are as disjointed and hard to follow when you're alone and not trying to talk to anyone?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, it is like that even when I’m by myself. That’s the part that makes it hard to explain, because it’s not only me failing to talk right in front of people. It starts in my head first.\n\nI’ll be sitting there alone, not even saying anything, and I’m trying to think about one thing — like, okay, where are my keys, or I have to get ready — and then it slides. Keys make me think of locks, and then locks make me think of jail, and then somehow I’m thinking about a prayer rug, and then chess pieces. And while it’s happening, it feels like there’s a reason those things are connected. It doesn’t feel random right away. It feels like I’m following something.\n\nBut then if I try to say it out loud later, it comes out all mixed up. That’s what brings me here, really — my talking gets tangled. I try to explain about the keys, then the jail, then the prayer rug, then chess pieces, and people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. And honestly, sometimes when I’m alone, even I lose the thread and can’t get it back straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble sticking to one topic when you're thinking to yourself, without trying to explain it to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when I’m not talking to anybody, it doesn’t stay on one thing. I’ll be trying to think about something ordinary, like where my keys are, and then it’s like my mind jumps tracks. Keys turns into locks, then I’m thinking about jail, then somehow a prayer rug comes in, then chess pieces, and I’m sitting there like… wait, how did I get over here?\n\nAt the time it feels connected, like there’s a path I’m following, but I can’t always explain the path after. And then when I do try to tell somebody, that’s when it really shows. What brings me here is my talking gets all mixed up. I try to explain about the keys, then the jail, then the prayer rug, then chess pieces, and people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. I’m not trying to wander off the subject, it just won’t stay in one place.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are as disjointed when you're not feeling pressured to explain them to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, even when nobody’s asking me anything, it can still be like that. It’s not only because I’m nervous or because someone is standing there waiting for me to explain it. It’s already mixed up before I open my mouth.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about something simple, just one thing, like the keys, and then it doesn’t stay there. And I can see it on their faces sometimes, like I lost them, but I don’t know exactly where I lost them because in my head it felt like it made sense a second before.\n\nDoctor: Do you have times when your thoughts and talking seem completely normal and organized, without any of the mixing up you've described?\n\nPatient: Sometimes I guess I can sound normal for a little bit, especially if it’s something simple, like yes or no, or if I’m just saying I cleaned this hallway or I need to go get something. My talking gets all mixed up. So yes, maybe there are little stretches where I seem okay, but then when I have to say what I mean, it can fall apart pretty fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have thoughts or beliefs that you think are true, but others might not agree with?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I know how it sounds when I say it, because other people don’t see it the same way, but it still feels true to me.\n\nAt work, in the building, I felt like the building people had marked me somehow. Not marked like with ink, I don’t mean that exactly, but like… I was singled out. Like they knew something about me or had decided something about me, and then the hallway lights and the way people looked at me were being used like signals. Little warnings. Like one light would flicker, or come on at a certain time, and then somebody would give me this quick look, not a normal look, more like they were passing it along.\n\nMy supervisor tried to show me the camera, like, “Look, nothing is happening, nobody is doing anything.” And I looked at it, I did. I stood there and watched what he was showing me. But even with that, I still felt sure there was something going on that just wasn’t showing up on the camera. Like the camera doesn’t catch what people mean by a look, or how the lights are timed, or the little things people do when they’re warning each other. So other people might say, “No, that’s not real,” but inside me it still felt real. It still does sometimes when I think about it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a period of time where your thoughts were completely clear and organized for several days in a row?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 53 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Thinking_Speech\nmanifestation: \"What brings me here is my talking gets all mixed up—I try to explain about the keys, then the jail, then the prayer rug, then chess pieces, and people tell me they cannot follow what I am saying.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Several days? I don’t know… not really, not in a way I could trust. I might have a few moments where I think, okay, I’m saying this straight, I’m making sense, but then when I try to explain more, it gets mixed up again.

That’s the main thing that brought me here, honestly. My talking gets all tangled. I start trying to explain one thing, like the keys, and then it turns into the jail, and then somehow I’m talking about the prayer rug, and then chess pieces, and I can see people’s faces change. They tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying, or they just look confused, and I’m still trying to get to the point, but it’s like the point moves away from me while I’m talking.

So if you mean completely clear and organized for days at a time… I can’t really say yes to that. Maybe little pieces, little stretches, but not several days where everything feels clear and stays that way.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess the main thing is my talking, it gets all mixed up, and people keep telling me they can’t follow me. Like I’ll be trying to explain something simple, you know, about the keys, because the keys matter, and then somehow I’m talking about jail, and then the prayer rug comes into it, and then chess pieces, and in my head it feels connected but when it comes out people look at me like I’m not making sense.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly. I try to slow down and say it right, but then I lose the start of what I was saying, or I jump to another part because that part seems important too. So I came in because it’s been bad enough that I can tell other people are getting confused by me, and I don’t know how to straighten it out when I’m speaking.\n\nDoctor: Have you been using any substances, like alcohol or drugs, around the times when your talking gets mixed up?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I haven’t been keeping track like that, like writing down what was happening before my talking got mixed up or anything. When people tell me I’m not making sense, I’m usually already in the middle of trying to explain something, so I’m not thinking, “What did I have earlier?” or connecting it to that.\n\nWhen you say substances, do you mean anything at all, like even small stuff, or do you mean more like being high or drunk? I just haven’t noticed a clear pattern where it’s like, “Oh, every time this happens, it’s because of that.” It feels more like the talking just starts going sideways, and then everybody’s reacting to me, and I’m trying to fix the sentence while it’s already coming out wrong.\n\nDoctor: Have you used any alcohol or drugs in the past month?\n\nPatient: In the past month… I’m not sure how to give you a solid answer. I mean, I do get… something, when I see that look on someone’s face, like they’re waiting for me to land the plane and I’m still circling around. It makes me more aware of myself, and then I start trying to fix it while I’m still talking, which doesn’t always help.\n\nAnxious, maybe, yes, in the sense that I feel pressure. It’s more like embarrassment and frustration mixed together. I can tell they’re not following me, and then I get stuck trying to explain the explanation, and that makes me sound even more tangled.\n\nSometimes I want to slow down, but then if I slow down too much I lose the piece I was trying to say. That’s the thing. It doesn’t feel like me thinking it. It sounds like it’s coming from somewhere in the building, like the vents, or once I thought it was from the mop closet, like somebody was in there but when I checked there wasn’t anybody. And it’ll say my name, or it’ll say something like I ruined everything.\n\nAnd when it happens, it sounds real. I mean real like if you were standing right beside me and said something. So then I get distracted because I’m trying to listen and also trying not to look like I’m listening to something nobody else hears, and then my talking gets worse because now I’m answering you, but part of me is checking the room, checking the vent, wondering if somebody’s messing with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to organize your thoughts even when you're not trying to explain something to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, it isn’t only when I’m talking to somebody. That’s the part that scares me a little, because even when I’m just by myself, like doing something simple, my mind starts lining things up in a way that feels connected to me, but then if I try to hold onto it, it gets all mixed up.\n\nLike the keys thing I mentioned — I’ll be thinking about keys because I need the keys for work, and then it’s locks, and then jail, because locks mean jail, and then somehow I’m thinking about a prayer rug, and then chess pieces, and I can kind of see the path between them in my head. It’s not just random to me while it’s happening. But if I had to say it out loud, people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. And sometimes even inside my own head, I lose which part was the main point.\n\nIt’s like I have too many beginnings and no straight hallway to walk down. I’m trying to organize it, but it keeps branching off. Sometimes I don’t get there, or I get there and I’m not doing things the way they want, or people say they can’t count on me the same way. And with cleaning, you know, people notice if something’s not done right. It’s not like you can hide it. Floors aren’t finished, trash isn’t handled, bathrooms aren’t stocked right, and then somebody has to come behind you. I’ve lost some of that work because of it. People stop calling, or they say it’s better if they use somebody else because they need someone reliable.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I stopped going to the mosque like I used to. I used to be more regular, and I’d see people, talk a little, be part of things. Now I stay away more. Same with family gatherings. I don’t go much, or I leave early, because it feels like people can’t follow me or they get uncomfortable, and then I feel like I’m making everybody tired. I’m not functioning the way I was.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe things that, in hindsight, might not be true, like thinking someone is out to get you or that you have special powers?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’ve had that. And then the little looks people gave each other, or somebody stopping talking when I came near… it felt like they were warning each other about me.\n\nMy supervisor even showed me the camera, like, “Look, nobody’s doing anything, nobody’s following you, nothing’s happening there.” And I could see what he was trying to show me. I could see the screen. But inside I still felt sure. That’s the part that embarrasses me, because even with him showing me nothing was on the camera, I didn’t believe it the way I should have. I kept thinking maybe the camera didn’t catch the right part, or they knew where the camera was, or it was happening in the lights and the way people looked, not something obvious.\n\nLater, when I’m calmer, I can say maybe that wasn’t true. And in my head, while it’s happening, it feels like there is a connection. I can feel the connection, almost like I’m following a thread. But when I say it out loud, people look at me like I skipped half the steps, or they tell me, “I can’t follow what you’re saying.”\n\nThat’s what brings me here, really. My talking gets all mixed up. It’s frustrating, because I’m not trying to confuse people. I’m trying to say it right. It just doesn’t stay straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you find your thoughts are as disjointed and hard to follow when you're alone and not trying to talk to anyone?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, it is like that even when I’m by myself. And while it’s happening, it feels like there’s a reason those things are connected. It doesn’t feel random right away. It feels like I’m following something.\n\nBut then if I try to say it out loud later, it comes out all mixed up. That’s what brings me here, really — my talking gets tangled. I try to explain about the keys, then the jail, then the prayer rug, then chess pieces, and people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. And honestly, sometimes when I’m alone, even I lose the thread and can’t get it back straight.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble sticking to one topic when you're thinking to yourself, without trying to explain it to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when I’m not talking to anybody, it doesn’t stay on one thing. I’ll be trying to think about something ordinary, like where my keys are, and then it’s like my mind jumps tracks. Keys turns into locks, then I’m thinking about jail, then somehow a prayer rug comes in, then chess pieces, and I’m sitting there like… wait, how did I get over here?\n\nAt the time it feels connected, like there’s a path I’m following, but I can’t always explain the path after. And then when I do try to tell somebody, that’s when it really shows. What brings me here is my talking gets all mixed up. I try to explain about the keys, then the jail, then the prayer rug, then chess pieces, and people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. I’m not trying to wander off the subject, it just won’t stay in one place.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are as disjointed when you're not feeling pressured to explain them to someone else?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, even when nobody’s asking me anything, it can still be like that. It’s not only because I’m nervous or because someone is standing there waiting for me to explain it. It’s already mixed up before I open my mouth.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about something simple, just one thing, like the keys, and then it doesn’t stay there. It goes from keys to jail, or locks and jail, and then somehow I’m thinking about a prayer rug, and then chess pieces, and while it’s happening I feel like there’s a connection. But then if I try to put it into words, it comes out wrong, like I’m jumping all over.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. My talking gets all mixed up. I try to explain about the keys, then the jail, then the prayer rug, then the chess pieces, and people tell me they can’t follow what I’m saying. And I can see it on their faces sometimes, like I lost them, but I don’t know exactly where I lost them because in my head it felt like it made sense a second before.\n\nDoctor: Do you have times when your thoughts and talking seem completely normal and organized, without any of the mixing up you've described?\n\nPatient: Sometimes I guess I can sound normal for a little bit, especially if it’s something simple, like yes or no, or if I’m just saying I cleaned this hallway or I need to go get something.
